**Saudi Arabia**,[^cite_note-20] officially the **Kingdom of Saudi Arabia** (**KSA**),[^cite_note-21] is a country in [West Asia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/West_Asia) that occupies the bulk of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md). Covering a land area of approximately 2,149,690 km<sup>2</sup> (830,000 sq mi), it is the [fifth-largest country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Asian_countries_by_area) in [Asia](689.md) and the [twelfth-largest](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) in the world. It is bordered by the [Red Sea](26191.md) to the west, [Jordan](7515964.md), [Iraq](7515928.md), and [Kuwait](7515890.md) to the north, the [Persian Gulf](24761.md), [Bahrain](18933277.md), [Qatar](8238258.md), and the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) to the east, [Oman](22316.md) to the southeast, and [Yemen](350939.md) to the south. The [Gulf of Aqaba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_of_Aqaba) in the northwest separates it from [Egypt](8087628.md) and [Israel](9282173.md).[^cite_note-22][^cite_note-23] [Riyadh](51554.md) is the capital and largest city, while other major centers include [Jeddah](277718.md) and the two holiest cities in [Islam](6037917.md), [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca) and [Medina](36636.md).

[Pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia), the territory that constitutes Saudi Arabia, was the site of several ancient cultures and civilizations; the [archaeological study](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Archaeology_of_the_Arabian_Peninsula) of the [prehistory of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prehistoric_Arabia) has identified some of the earliest traces of human activity outside [Africa](5334607.md).[^cite_note-24] [Islam](6037917.md) emerged in what is now Saudi Arabia in the early [seventh century](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/7th_century). Islamic prophet [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) united the population of the Arabian Peninsula and created a single Islamic religious polity. Following his death in 632, his followers expanded Muslim rule beyond Arabia, [conquering](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Early_Muslim_conquests) territories in [North Africa](21714.md), [Central](6742.md), [South Asia](21566765.md) and [Iberia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iberian_Peninsula) within decades.[^cite_note-Abbas-25][^cite_note-Reichl-26][^cite_note-Barber-27] Arab dynasties originating from modern-day Saudi Arabia founded the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) (632–661), [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) (661–750), [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) (750–1517), and [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) (909–1171) caliphates, as well as [numerous other Muslim states](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_Muslim_states_and_dynasties) in Asia, [Africa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Africa#History), and [Europe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Europe#Western_Europe_and_the_Mediterranean_Region).

Saudi Arabia was founded in 1932 by [King Abdulaziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Saud), who united [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz), [Najd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Najd), parts of [Eastern Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia) and [South Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/South_Arabia) into a single state through [a series of military and political campaigns](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Formation_of_Saudi_Arabia) beginning in 1902. The Kingdom is the [Third Saudi State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Third_Saudi_state) and the successor to the [First Saudi State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/First_Saudi_state) (1727–1818) and the [Second Saudi State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_Saudi_state) (1824–1891). The country has since been governed as an [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy) under the [House of Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud).[^cite_note-28] In its [Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_of_Saudi_Arabia), Saudi Arabia defines itself as a sovereign Arab [Islamic state](2646438.md) with [Islam as its official religion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islam_in_Saudi_Arabia) and [Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) as its official language. The ultraconservative [Wahhabi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism) religious movement within [Sunni Islam](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunni_Islam) was the prevailing political and cultural force until the 2000s.[^cite_note-HT2003:_14-29][^cite_note-ReferenceA-30] The Saudi government has attracted criticism for various policies such as its [intervention in the Yemeni Civil War](46230181.md), restrictions on political and civil liberties, and poor [human rights record](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-31][^cite_note-32][^cite_note-33] Since the mid-2010s, Saudi Arabia has instituted limited and gradual reforms to liberalize social, cultural, and religious life.[^cite_note-34]

Saudi Arabia is considered both a [regional](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regional_power) and [middle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Middle_power) power,[^cite_note-The_United_States_and_the_Great_Powers-35][^cite_note-36] in addition to being a [major non-NATO ally](3812445.md) of the [United States](3434750.md).[^cite_note-37] Since [petroleum](23195.md) was [discovered in the country in 1938](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dammam_No._7),[^cite_note-38][^cite_note-39] the kingdom has become the world's [second-largest oil producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_extraction) and leading [oil exporter](17808102.md), controlling the world's [second-largest oil reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves#Countries) and [sixth-largest gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves).[^cite_note-\:0-40] Saudi Arabia is categorized as a [World Bank high-income economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/High-income_economy) and is the only Arab country among the [G20](2206608.md) major economies.[^cite_note-41][^cite_note-42] The [Saudi economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Saudi_Arabia) is the [largest in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Middle_East) and the world's [nineteenth-largest by nominal GDP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(nominal\)) and [seventeenth-largest by PPP](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_GDP_\(PPP\)). Ranking very high in the [Human Development Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_Development_Index),[^cite_note-UNDR2014-43] Saudi Arabia offers [free university tuition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Higher_education_in_Saudi_Arabia), no personal income tax,[^cite_note-44] and [free universal health care](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Health_care_in_Saudi_Arabia). With its [dependence on foreign labour](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_workers_in_Saudi_Arabia), Saudi Arabia has the world's [third-largest immigrant population](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sovereign_states_by_immigrant_and_emigrant_population), with foreign-born residents comprising roughly 40% of the population.[^cite_note-45] [Saudi Arabians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudis) are among [the world's youngest people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_in_Saudi_Arabia), with approximately half being under 25 years old.[^cite_note-46][^cite_note-47] Saudi Arabia is a member of the [Gulf Cooperation Council](220950.md), [United Nations](31769.md), [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md), [Arab League](52625.md), and [OPEC](166346.md), as well as a dialogue partner of the [Shanghai Cooperation Organisation](152422.md).

## Etymology

See also: [House of Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud) and [Arab (etymology)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_\(etymology\))

Following the amalgamation of the [Kingdom of Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hejaz) and [Nejd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Nejd), [Abdulaziz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Saud) issued a royal decree on 23 September 1932 naming the new state *al-Mamlaka al-ʿArabiyya as-Suʿūdiyya* ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic) المملكة العربية السعودية), which is normally translated as "the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia" in English,[^cite_note-US_State_Dept_Saudi_Arabia-48] but literally means "the **Saudi Arab Kingdom**",[^cite_note-49] or "the **Saudi Kingdom of Arabia**" (compare [the Hashemite Kingdom of Jordan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Hashemite_Kingdom_of_Jordan)).

The word "Saudi" is derived from the element *as-Suʿūdīyya* in the Arabic name of the country, which is a type of adjective known as a *[nisba](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nisba_\(onomastics\)),* formed from the dynastic name of the Saudi royal family, the [Al Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): آل سعود). Its inclusion expresses the view that the country is the personal possession of the royal family.[^cite_note-50][^cite_note-Kamrava-51] *Al Saud* is an [Arabic name](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_name) formed by adding the word *Al*, meaning "family of" or "House of",[^cite_note-52] to the personal name of an ancestor. In the case of Al Saud, this is [Saud ibn Muhammad ibn Muqrin](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saud_ibn_Muhammad_ibn_Muqrin), the father of the dynasty's 18th-century founder, [Muhammad bin Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_bin_Saud).[^cite_note-53]

## History

Main article: [History of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_Saudi_Arabia)

### Prehistory



Anthropomorphic stela (4th millennium BCE) from El-Maakir-Qaryat al-Kaafa

There is evidence that human habitation in the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) dates back to about 125,000 years ago.[^cite_note-54] A 2011 study found that the first modern humans to spread east across Asia left [Africa](5334607.md) about 75,000 years ago across the [Bab-el-Mandeb](62138.md) connecting the [Horn of Africa](62142.md) and Arabia.[^cite_note-55] The Arabian Peninsula is regarded as central to the understanding of evolution and dispersals of humanity. Arabia underwent an extreme environmental fluctuation in the [Quaternary](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quaternary) that led to profound evolutionary and demographic changes. Arabia's rich [Lower Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lower_Paleolithic) record, marked by numerous [Oldowan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oldowan)-like sites, indicates a significant role in the early hominin colonization of [Eurasia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eurasia).[^cite_note-56]

In the [Neolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Neolithic) period, prominent cultures such as [Al-Magar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Magar), whose centre lay in modern-day southwestern Najd, flourished. Al-Magar could be considered a "Neolithic Revolution" in human knowledge and handicraft skills.[^cite_note-57] The culture is characterized as being one of the world's first to involve the widespread domestication of animals, particularly the horse.[^cite_note-58][^cite_note-59] Al-Magar statues were made from local stone, and it seems that the statues were fixed in a central building that might have had a significant role in the social and religious life of the inhabitants.[^cite_note-60]

In 2017, hunting scenes showing images of most likely domesticated dogs (resembling the [Canaan Dog](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Canaan_Dog)) were discovered in northwestern Saudi Arabia. These rock engravings date back more than 8,000 years, making them the earliest depictions of dogs in the world.[^cite_note-61]

At the end of the 4th millennium BCE, Arabia entered the [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age); metals were widely used, and the period was characterized by its 2 m high burials which were simultaneously followed by the existence of numerous temples that included many free-standing sculptures originally painted with red colours.[^cite_note-62]

In May 2021, archaeologists announced that a 350,000-year-old [Acheulean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acheulean) site named An Nasim in the [Hail region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/%E1%B8%A4a%CA%BCil_Province) could be the oldest human habitation site in northern Saudi Arabia. [Paleolithic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Paleolithic) artefacts are similar to material remains uncovered at the Acheulean sites in the [Nefud Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/An_Nafud).[^cite_note-63][^cite_note-64][^cite_note-65][^cite_note-66]

### Pre-Islamic

Main article: [Pre-Islamic Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pre-Islamic_Arabia)



The "Worshipping Servant" statue (2500 BCE), above one metre (3 ft 3 in) in height, is much taller than any possible Mesopotamian or Harappan models. Photo courtesy of the [National Museum of Korea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Museum_of_Korea).

[^cite_note-Roads_of_Arabia_p._180-67]

The earliest sedentary culture in Saudi Arabia dates to the [Ubaid period](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ubaid_period) at [Dosariyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dosariyah). Climatic change and the onset of aridity may have brought about the end of this phase of settlement, as little archaeological evidence exists from the succeeding millennium.[^cite_note-68] The settlement of the region picks up again in the period of [Dilmun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dilmun) in the early 3rd millennium. Known records from [Uruk](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uruk) refer to a place called Dilmun, associated on several occasions with copper, and in later periods it was a source of imported woods in southern Mesopotamia. Scholars have suggested that Dilmun originally designated the [Eastern Province](468721.md), notably linked with the major Dilmunite settlements of Umm an-Nussi and Umm ar-Ramadh in the interior and Tarout on the coast. It is likely that [Tarout Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tarout_Island) was the main port and the capital of Dilmun.[^cite_note-Roads_of_Arabia_p._180-67] Mesopotamian inscribed clay tablets suggest that, in the early period of Dilmun, a hierarchical organized political structure existed. In 1966, an earthwork in Tarout exposed an ancient burial field that yielded a large statue dating to the Dilmunite period (mid 3rd millennium BCE). The statue was locally made under the strong Mesopotamian influence on the artistic principle of Dilmun.[^cite_note-Roads_of_Arabia_p._180-67]

By 2200 BCE, the centre of Dilmun shifted for unknown reasons from Tarout and the Saudi Arabian mainland to the island of [Bahrain](18933277.md), and a highly developed settlement emerged there, where a laborious temple complex and thousands of burial mounds dating to this period were discovered.[^cite_note-Roads_of_Arabia_p._180-67]



*Qaṣr Al-Farīd*, the largest of the 131 [rock-cut](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rock_cut_architecture) monumental tombs built from the 1st century BCE to the 1st century CE, with their elaborately ornamented façades, at the extensive ancient [Nabatean](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabatean) archaeological site of [Hegra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hegra_\(Mada'in_Salih\)) located in the area of [Al-'Ula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-'Ula) within [Al Madinah Region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Madinah_Region) in the [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz). A UNESCO [World Heritage Site](44940.md) since 2008.

By the late [Bronze Age](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bronze_Age), a historically recorded people and land ([Midian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midian) and the Midianites) in the north-western portion of Saudi Arabia are well-documented in the Bible. Centred in [Tabouk](900737.md), it stretched from [Wadi Arabah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadi_Arabah) in the north to the area of [al-Wajh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Wajh) in the south.[^cite_note-69] The capital of Midian was Qurayyah,[^cite_note-ArnoldStrawn2016-70] it consists of a large, fortified citadel encompassing 35 hectares and below it lies a walled settlement of 15 hectares. The city hosted as many as 12,000 inhabitants.[^cite_note-Coogan2001-71] The Bible recounts [Israel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Israel_\(united_monarchy\))'s [two wars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midian_war) with Midian, in the early 11th century BCE. Politically, the Midianites were described as having a decentralized structure headed by five kings (Evi, Rekem, Tsur, Hur, and Reba); the names appear to be toponyms of important Midianite settlements.[^cite_note-72] It is common to view that Midian designated a confederation of tribes, the sedentary element settled in the Hijaz while its nomadic affiliates pastured and sometimes pillaged as far away as [Palestine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestine_\(region\)).[^cite_note-Midian_p._163-73] The nomadic [Midianites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Midianites) were one of the earliest exploiters of the domestication of camels that enabled them to navigate through the harsh terrains of the region.[^cite_note-Midian_p._163-73]



Colossal statue from [Al-'Ula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-'Ula) in the [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz) (6th–4th century BCE); it followed the standardized artistic sculpting of the [Lihyanite](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihyan) kingdom.

[^cite_note-74]

At the end of the 7th century BCE, an emerging kingdom appeared in north-western Arabia. It started as a sheikdom of Dedan, which developed into the kingdom of [Lihyan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Lihyan).[^cite_note-Lihyan_p._192-75][^cite_note-76] During this period, Dedan transformed into a kingdom that encompassed a much wider domain.[^cite_note-Lihyan_p._192-75] In the early 3rd century BCE, with bustling economic activity between the south and north, Lihyan acquired large influence suitable to its strategic position on the caravan road.[^cite_note-77] The Lihyanites ruled over a large domain from [Yathrib](36636.md) in the south and parts of the Levant in the north.[^cite_note-78] In antiquity, Gulf of Aqaba used to be called Gulf of Lihyan, a testimony to the extensive influence that Lihyan acquired.[^cite_note-HiltonHilton1996-79]

The Lihyanites fell into the hands of the [Nabataeans](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nabataeans) around 65 BCE upon their seizure of Hegra then marching to [Tayma](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tayma), and to their capital Dedan in 9 BCE. The Nabataeans ruled large portions of north Arabia until their domain was annexed by the [Roman Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Roman_Empire), which renamed it [Arabia Petraea](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabia_Petraea), and remained under the rule of the Romans until 630.[^cite_note-80]

### Middle Ages and rise of Islam

Main article: [Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Caliphate)



At its greatest extent, the [Umayyad Caliphate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) (661–750) covered 11100000 km

2

&#x20;(4300000 sq mi)

[^cite_note-81]

&#x20;and 62 million people (29 per cent of the world's population),

[^cite_note-Blankinship-82]

&#x20;making it one of the [largest empires in history](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_largest_empires) in both area and proportion of the world's population. It was also larger than any previous empire in history.

Shortly before the advent of Islam, apart from urban trading settlements (such as Mecca and Medina), much of what was to become Saudi Arabia was populated by nomadic pastoral tribal societies.[^cite_note-83] Muhammad was born in Mecca in about 570 [CE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Common_Era). In the early 7th century, [Muhammad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad) united the [various tribes of the peninsula](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribes_of_Arabia) and created a single Islamic religious polity.[^cite_note-James_E._Lindsay_2005_33-84] Following his death in 632, his followers expanded the territory under Muslim rule beyond Arabia, conquering territory in the Iberian Peninsula in the west, to parts of Central and South Asia in the east, in a matter of decades.[^cite_note-Abbas-25][^cite_note-Reichl-26][^cite_note-Barber-27] Arabia became a more politically peripheral region of the Muslim world as the focus shifted to the newly conquered lands.[^cite_note-James_E._Lindsay_2005_33-84]

[Arabs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) originating from modern-day Saudi Arabia, the [Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hejaz) in particular, founded the [Rashidun](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidun_Caliphate) (632–661), [Umayyad](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Umayyad_Caliphate) (661–750), [Abbasid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abbasid_Caliphate) (750–1517), and the [Fatimid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fatimid_Caliphate) (909–1171) caliphates. From the 10th century to the early 20th century, Mecca and Medina were under the control of a local Arab ruler known as the [Sharif of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_of_Mecca), but at most times the Sharif owed allegiance to the ruler of one of the major Islamic empires based in [Baghdad](4492.md), [Cairo](6293.md) or [Istanbul](3391396.md). Most of the remainder of what became Saudi Arabia reverted to traditional tribal rule.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85][^cite_note-86]



The [Battle of Badr](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Badr), 13 March 624 CE

For much of the 10th century, the [Isma'ili](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Isma'ilism)-Shi'ite [Qarmatians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qarmatians) were the most powerful force in the Persian Gulf. In 930, the Qarmatians pillaged Mecca, outraging the Muslim world, particularly with their theft of the [Black Stone](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Black_Stone).[^cite_note-87] In 1077–1078, an Arab sheikh named [Abdullah bin Ali Al Uyuni](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_bin_Ali_Al_Uyuni) defeated the Qarmatians in [Bahrain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia) and [al-Hasa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Ahsa_Oasis) with the help of the [Seljuq Empire](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Seljuk_Empire) and founded the [Uyunid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyunid_dynasty).[^cite_note-88][^cite_note-89] The [Uyunid Emirate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Uyunid_Emirate) later underwent expansion, with its territory stretching from Najd to the [Syrian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Syrian_Desert).[^cite_note-Safa_Khulusi-90] They were overthrown by the [Usfurids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Usfurids) in 1253.[^cite_note-91] Usfurid rule was weakened after Persian rulers of [Hormuz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ormus) captured Bahrain and [Qatif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qatif) in 1320.[^cite_note-92] The vassals of Ormuz, the Shia [Jarwanid dynasty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jarwanid_dynasty) came to rule eastern Arabia in the 14th century.[^cite_note-JuanCole-93][^cite_note-94] The [Jabrids](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabrids) took control of the region after overthrowing the Jarwanids in the 15th century and clashed with Hormuz for more than two decades over the region for its economic revenues, until finally agreeing to pay [tribute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tribute) in 1507.[^cite_note-JuanCole-93] [Al-Muntafiq](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Muntafiq) tribe later took over the region and came under [Ottoman](22278.md) [suzerainty](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Suzerainty). The [Bani Khalid tribe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bani_Khalid_\(tribe\)) later revolted against them in the 17th century and took control.[^cite_note-95] Their rule extended from Iraq to Oman at its height, and they too came under Ottoman suzerainty.[^cite_note-96][^cite_note-97]

### Ottoman Hejaz and Portuguese era

Main articles: [Ottoman era in the history of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman_era_in_the_history_of_Saudi_Arabia), [Mamluk–Portuguese conflicts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mamluk%E2%80%93Portuguese_conflicts), and [Ottoman–Portuguese confrontations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Portuguese_confrontations)

In the 16th century, the Ottomans added the Red Sea and Persian Gulf coast (the Hejaz, [Aseer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aseer) and [Al-Ahsa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Eastern_Arabia)) to the empire and claimed suzerainty over the interior. One reason was to [thwart Portuguese attempts](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ottoman%E2%80%93Portuguese_conflicts_\(1538%E2%80%931560\)) to attack the Red Sea (hence the Hejaz) and the Indian Ocean.[^cite_note-Bernstein-98] The Ottoman degree of control over these lands varied over the next four centuries with the fluctuating strength or weakness of the empire's central authority.[^cite_note-99][^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowen200768-100] These changes contributed to later uncertainties, such as the dispute with Transjordan over the [inclusion of the sanjak of Ma'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Occupation_of_Ma'an), including the cities of [Ma'an](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ma'an) and [Aqaba](328399.md).

### Saud dynasty and unification

See also: [Unification of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Unification_of_Saudi_Arabia)



Expansion of the [first Saudi State](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Diriyah) in 1810

The emergence of what was to become the Saudi royal family, known as the Al Saud, began at the town of [Diriyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diriyah) in central Arabia with the accession as [emir](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emir) of [Muhammad bin Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_bin_Saud) on 22 February 1727.[^cite_note-101][^cite_note-102] In 1744 he joined forces with the religious leader [Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Abd_al-Wahhab),[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowen200769–70-103] founder of the Wahhabi movement, a strict puritanical form of Sunni Islam.[^cite_note-104] This alliance provided the ideological impetus to Saudi expansion and remains the basis of Saudi Arabian dynastic rule today.[^cite_note-105]

The [Emirate of Diriyah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Diriyah) established in the area around Riyadh rapidly expanded and briefly controlled most of the present-day territory of Saudi Arabia, [sacking Karbala](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabi_sack_of_Karbala) in 1802, and [capturing Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Destruction_of_early_Islamic_heritage_sites_in_Saudi_Arabia) in 1803. In 1818, it was destroyed by the Ottoman viceroy of Egypt, [Mohammed Ali Pasha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_Ali_of_Egypt).[^cite_note-106] The much smaller [Emirate of Nejd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Nejd) was established in 1824. Throughout the rest of the 19th century, the Al Saud contested control of the interior of what was to become Saudi Arabia with another Arabian ruling family, the [Al Rashid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rashidi_dynasty), who ruled the [Emirate of Jabal Shammar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Jabal_Shammar). By 1891, the Al Rashid were victorious and the Al Saud were driven into exile in Kuwait.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85]



British Prime Minister [Winston Churchill](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Winston_Churchill) and [Ibn Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Saud), the founding father and first king of Saudi Arabia, in [Egypt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Egypt) in 1945

At the beginning of the 20th century, the Ottoman Empire continued to control or have a suzerainty over most of the peninsula. Subject to this suzerainty, Arabia was ruled by a patchwork of tribal rulers,[^cite_note-Murphy-107][^cite_note-108] with the [Sharif of Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sharif_of_Mecca) having pre-eminence and ruling the Hejaz.[^cite_note-109] In 1902, [Abdul Rahman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdul_Rahman_bin_Faisal)'s son, Abdulaziz—later known as [Ibn Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ibn_Saud)—recaptured control of Riyadh bringing the Al Saud back to Nejd, creating the [third "Saudi state"](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emirate_of_Nejd_and_Hasa).[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] Ibn Saud gained the support of the [Ikhwan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ikhwan), a tribal army inspired by Wahhabism and led by [Faisal Al-Dawish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_Al-Dawish), and which had grown quickly after its foundation in 1912.[^cite_note-110] With the aid of the Ikhwan, Ibn Saud captured Al-Ahsa from the Ottomans in 1913.

In 1916, with the encouragement and support of [Britain](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Kingdom_of_Great_Britain_and_Ireland) (which was fighting the Ottomans in [World War I](4764461.md)), the Sharif of Mecca, [Hussein bin Ali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hussein_bin_Ali,_Sharif_of_Mecca), led a pan-[Arab Revolt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Revolt) against the Ottoman Empire to create a united Arab state.[^cite_note-111] Although the revolt failed in its objective, [the Allied](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allies_of_World_War_I) victory in World War I resulted in the end of Ottoman suzerainty and control in Arabia, and Hussein bin Ali became [King of Hejaz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kingdom_of_Hejaz).[^cite_note-112]

Ibn Saud avoided involvement in the Arab Revolt and instead continued his struggle with the Al Rashid. Following the latter's final defeat, he took the title [Sultan of Nejd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sultanate_of_Nejd) in 1921. With the help of the Ikhwan, the Kingdom of Hejaz was conquered in 1924–25, and on 10 January 1926, Ibn Saud declared himself king of Hejaz.[^cite_note-113] For the next five years, he administered the two parts of his dual kingdom as separate units.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85]

After the conquest of the Hejaz, the Ikhwan leadership's objective switched to expansion of the Wahhabist realm into the British protectorates of Transjordan, Iraq and Kuwait, and began raiding those territories. This met with Ibn Saud's opposition, as he recognized the danger of a direct conflict with the British. At the same time, the Ikhwan became disenchanted with Ibn Saud's domestic policies which appeared to favour modernization and the increase in the number of non-Muslim foreigners in the country. As a result, they turned against Ibn Saud and, after a two-year struggle, were defeated in 1929 at the [Battle of Sabilla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Battle_of_Sabilla), where their leaders were massacred.[^cite_note-114] On Ibn Saud's behalf, [Prince Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_bin_Abdulaziz_al_Saud) declared the unification on 23 September 1932, and the two kingdoms of Hejaz and Nejd were unified as the Kingdom of Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] That date is now a national holiday called [Saudi National Day](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_National_Day).[^cite_note-115]

### 20th century

Main article: [Modern history of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Modern_history_of_Saudi_Arabia)



The oil well *[Dammam No. 7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dammam_No._7)* on March 4, 1938, the day it struck oil in commercial quantities, becoming the first in Saudi Arabia to do so

&#x20;The new kingdom was reliant on limited agriculture and pilgrimage revenues.[^cite_note-116] [In 1938, vast reserves of oil were discovered](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dammam_No._7) in the Al-Ahsa region along the coast of the Persian Gulf, and full-scale development of the oil fields began in 1941 under the US-controlled [Aramco (Arabian American Oil Company)](290527.md). Oil provided Saudi Arabia with economic prosperity and substantial political leverage internationally.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] Cultural life rapidly developed, primarily in the Hejaz, which was the centre for newspapers and radio. However, the large influx of [foreign workers in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_workers_in_Saudi_Arabia) in the oil industry increased the pre-existing propensity for [xenophobia](34141.md). At the same time, the government became increasingly wasteful and extravagant. By the 1950s this had led to large governmental deficits and excessive foreign borrowing.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] In 1953, [Saud of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saud_of_Saudi_Arabia) succeeded as the king of Saudi Arabia. In 1964 he was deposed in favour of his half brother [Faisal of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_of_Saudi_Arabia), after an intense rivalry, fuelled by doubts in the royal family over Saud's competence. In 1972, Saudi Arabia gained a 20% control in Aramco, thereby decreasing US control over Saudi oil.[^cite_note-BBC_timeline-117] In 1973, Saudi Arabia led an [oil boycott](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) against the Western countries that supported Israel in the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War) against Egypt and Syria, leading to the quadrupling of oil prices.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] In 1975, Faisal was assassinated by his nephew, Prince [Faisal bin Musaid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_bin_Musaid) and was succeeded by his half-brother [King Khalid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_of_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-Al-Rasheed_136-137-118]

Nasser and Faisal

Faisal (left) with Egypt's President [Gamal Abdel Nasser](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gamal_Abdel_Nasser) in [Cairo](6293.md), 1969

By 1976, Saudi Arabia had become the largest oil producer in the world.[^cite_note-119] Khalid's reign saw economic and social development progress at an extremely rapid rate, transforming the infrastructure and educational system of the country;[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] in foreign policy, close ties with the US were developed.[^cite_note-Al-Rasheed_136-137-118] In 1979, two events occurred which greatly concerned the government[^cite_note-120] and had a long-term influence on Saudi foreign and domestic policy. The first was the [Iranian Islamic Revolution](347268.md). It was feared that the country's [Shi'ite minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shi'a_Islam_in_Saudi_Arabia) in the Eastern Province (which is also the location of the oil fields) might rebel under the influence of their Iranian co-religionists. There were several anti-government uprisings in the region such as the [1979 Qatif Uprising](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1979_Qatif_Uprising).[^cite_note-Toby_Craig_Jones_2010_218–219-121] The second event was the [Grand Mosque Seizure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_Seizure) in Mecca by Islamist extremists. The militants involved were in part angered by what they considered to be the corruption and un-Islamic nature of the Saudi government.[^cite_note-Toby_Craig_Jones_2010_218–219-121] The government regained control of the mosque after 10 days, and those captured were executed. Part of the response of the royal family was to enforce the much stricter observance of traditional religious and social norms in the country (for example, the closure of cinemas) and to give the [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulema) a greater role in government.[^cite_note-Hegghammer24-122] Neither entirely succeeded as Islamism continued to grow in strength.[^cite_note-123]



Map of Saudi Arabian administrative regions and roadways

In 1980, Saudi Arabia bought out the American interests in Aramco.[^cite_note-124] King Khalid died in 1982 and was succeeded by his brother, [King Fahd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahd_of_Saudi_Arabia). Fahd continued to develop close relations with the United States and increased the purchase of American and British military equipment.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] The vast wealth generated by oil revenues was beginning to have an even greater impact on Saudi society. It led to rapid technological (but not cultural) modernization, urbanization, mass public education, and the creation of new media. This and the presence of increasingly large numbers of foreign workers greatly affected traditional Saudi norms and values. Although there was a dramatic change in the social and economic life of the country, political power continued to be monopolized by the royal family[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] leading to discontent among many Saudis who began to look for wider participation in government.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbir1993114-125]

In the 1980s, Saudi Arabia and [Kuwait](7515890.md) spent \$25 billion in support of [Saddam Hussein](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saddam_Hussein) in the [Iran–Iraq War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Iraq_War) (1980–1988);[^cite_note-126] however, Saudi Arabia condemned the [invasion of Kuwait](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Invasion_of_Kuwait) in 1990 and asked the United States to intervene.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] King Fahd allowed American and coalition troops to be stationed in Saudi Arabia. He invited the Kuwaiti government and many of its citizens to stay in Saudi Arabia, but expelled citizens of [Yemen](350939.md) and [Jordan](7515964.md) because of their governments' support of [Iraq](26215470.md). In 1991, Saudi Arabian forces were involved both in bombing raids on Iraq and in the land invasion that helped to liberate Kuwait, which became known as the [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War) (1990–1991).[^cite_note-BBC_timeline-117]

Saudi Arabia's relations with the West was one of the issues that led to [an increase in Islamist terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_militant_incidents_in_Saudi_Arabia) in Saudi Arabia, as well as Islamist terrorist attacks in Western countries by Saudi nationals. [Osama bin Laden](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Osama_bin_Laden) was a Saudi citizen (until stripped of his citizenship in 1994) and was responsible for the [1998 U.S. embassy bombings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1998_United_States_embassy_bombings) in East Africa and the 2000 [USS *Cole* bombing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/USS_Cole_bombing) near the port of [Aden](38050.md), Yemen. 15 of [the hijackers](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hijackers_in_the_September_11_attacks) involved in the [September 11 attacks](5058690.md) were Saudi nationals.[^cite_note-127] Many Saudis who did not support the Islamist terrorists were nevertheless deeply unhappy with the government's policies.[^cite_note-128]

Islamism was not the only source of hostility to the government. Although extremely wealthy by the 21st century, Saudi Arabia's economy was near stagnant. High taxes and a growth in unemployment have contributed to discontent and have been reflected in a rise in civil unrest, and discontent with the royal family. In response, a number of limited reforms were initiated by King Fahd. In March 1992, he introduced the "[Basic Law](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_of_Saudi_Arabia)", which emphasized the duties and responsibilities of a ruler. In December 1993, the Consultative Council was inaugurated. It is composed of a chairman and 60 members—all chosen by the King. Fahd made it clear that he did not have democracy in mind, saying: "A system based on elections is not consistent with our Islamic creed, which \[approves of] government by consultation \[shūrā]."[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85]

In 1995, Fahd suffered a stroke, and the Crown Prince, [Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_of_Saudi_Arabia), became *de facto* regent; however, his authority was hindered by conflict with Fahd's full brothers (known, with Fahd, as the "[Sudairi Seven](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sudairi_Seven)").[^cite_note-129]

### 21st century

Signs of discontent included, in 2003 and 2004, a series of bombings and armed violence in Riyadh, Jeddah, Yanbu and Khobar.[^cite_note-Cordesman_2009-130] In February–April 2005, [the first-ever nationwide municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2005_Saudi_Arabian_municipal_elections) were held in Saudi Arabia. Women were not allowed to take part.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85]

In 2005, King Fahd died and was succeeded by Abdullah, who continued the policy of minimum reform and clamping down on protests. The king introduced economic reforms aimed at reducing the country's reliance on oil revenue: limited [deregulation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deregulation), encouragement of foreign investment, and [privatization](24661.md). In February 2009, Abdullah announced a series of governmental changes to the judiciary, armed forces, and various ministries to modernize these institutions including the replacement of senior appointees in the judiciary and the [Mutaween](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_religious_police) (religious police) with more moderate individuals and the appointment of the country's first female deputy minister.[^cite_note-Britannica_history-85] On 29 January 2011, hundreds of protesters gathered in [Jeddah](277718.md) in a rare display of criticism against the city's poor infrastructure after flooding killed 11 people.[^cite_note-Montreal-131]



[Egyptian President](335341.md) [Abdel Fattah el-Sisi](36703624.md) with King Salman, U.S. President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and First Lady [Melania](1209075.md) participating in the inaugural opening of the [Global Center to Combat Extremism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Global_Center_to_Combat_Extremism), Sunday, May 21, 2017

Since 2011, Saudi Arabia has been affected by its own [Arab Spring protests](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2011%E2%80%9313_Saudi_Arabian_protests).[^cite_note-Fisk_troops-132] In response, King Abdullah announced on 22 February 2011 a series of benefits for citizens amounting to \$36 billion, of which \$10.7 billion was earmarked for housing.[^cite_note-133][^cite_note-134][^cite_note-135] No political reforms were included, though some prisoners indicted for financial crimes were pardoned.[^cite_note-AlJazeeraEnglishBenefits-136] Abdullah also announced a package of \$93 billion, which included 500,000 new homes to a cost of \$67 billion, in addition to creating 60000 new security jobs.[^cite_note-theguardian1-137][^cite_note-138] Although male-only [municipal elections were held in 2011](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_municipal_elections,_2011),[^cite_note-alawsat_details-139][^cite_note-bloom_women-140] Abdullah allowed women to vote and be elected in the [2015 municipal elections](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_municipal_elections,_2015), and also to be nominated to the [Shura Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consultative_Assembly_of_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-oman_observer_electionday-141]

## Geography

Main article: [Geography of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Geography_of_Saudi_Arabia)



Saudi Arabia topography



[Harrat Khaybar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Harrat_Khaybar) seen from the [International Space Station](15043.md). Saudi Arabia is home to more than 2000 dormant volcanoes.

[^cite_note-auto1-142]

&#x20;Lava fields in Hejaz, known locally by their Arabic name of harrat (the singular is harrah), form one of Earth's largest [alkali basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_basalt) regions, covering some 180,000 square kilometres (69,000 sq mi).

[^cite_note-auto-143]

Saudi Arabia occupies about 80% of the [Arabian Peninsula](47858.md) (the world's largest peninsula),[^cite_note-Stokes605-144] lying between latitudes [16°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/16th_parallel_north) and [33° N](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/33rd_parallel_north), and longitudes [34°](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/34th_meridian_east) and [56° E](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/56th_meridian_east). Because the country's southeastern and southern borders with the [United Arab Emirates](69328.md) and [Oman](22316.md) are not precisely marked, the exact size of the country is undefined.[^cite_note-Stokes605-144] The [United Nations Statistics Division](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/United_Nations_Statistics_Division) estimates 2149690 km<sup>2</sup> (830000 sq mi) and lists Saudi Arabia as the world's [12th](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_and_dependencies_by_area) largest state. It is geographically the largest country in the Middle East and on the [Arabian Plate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Plate).[^cite_note-145]

Saudi Arabia's geography is dominated by the [Arabian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Desert), associated semi-desert, shrubland, steppes, several mountain ranges, volcanic lava fields and highlands. The 647500 km<sup>2</sup> (250001 sq mi) [Rub' al Khali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rub'_al_Khali) ("Empty Quarter") in the southeastern part of the country is the world's largest contiguous sand desert.[^cite_note-Britannica-146][^cite_note-147] Saudi Arabia is the largest country in the world without permanent rivers. The nation features several endorheic lakes, but its hydrology relies primarily on ephemeral watercourses known as [wadis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wadis), which [are numerous throughout the kingdom](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wadis_of_Saudi_Arabia). The fertile areas are to be found in the alluvial deposits in wadis, basins, and oases.[^cite_note-Britannica-146] There are [approximately 1,300 islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_islands_of_Saudi_Arabia) in the Red Sea and Persian Gulf.[^cite_note-148]

The main topographical feature is the central plateau which rises abruptly from the Red Sea and gradually descends into the [Nejd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nejd) and toward the Persian Gulf. On the Red Sea coast, there is a narrow coastal plain, known as the [Tihamah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tihamah), parallel to which runs along an imposing escarpment. The southwest province of [Aseer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Aseer) is mountainous and contains the 3015 m (9892 ft) [Jabal Soudah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jabal_Soudah), which is the highest point in the country.[^cite_note-Britannica-146] Saudi Arabia is home to more than 2,000 dormant volcanoes.[^cite_note-auto1-142] Lava fields in Hejaz, known locally by their Arabic name of harrat (the singular is harrah), form one of Earth's largest [alkali basalt](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alkali_basalt) regions, covering some 180,000 square kilometres (69,000 sq mi).[^cite_note-auto-143]

Except for the southwestern regions such as Aseer, Saudi Arabia has a [desert climate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Desert_climate) with very high day-time temperatures during the summer and a sharp temperature drop at night. Average summer temperatures are around 45 °C (113 °F) but can be as high as 54 °C (129 °F). In the winter the temperature rarely drops below 0 °C (32 °F) with the exception of mostly the northern regions of the country where annual snowfall, in particular in the mountainous regions of [Tabuk Province](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tabuk_Province), is not uncommon.[^cite_note-149] The lowest recorded temperature, −12.0 °C (10.4 °F), was measured in [Turaif](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Turaif).[^cite_note-SaudiCC-150] Of the Gulf states, Saudi Arabia is likely to experience snowfalls most frequently.[^cite_note-151]

In the spring and autumn the heat is temperate, temperatures average around 29 °C (84 °F). Annual rainfall is very low. The southern regions differ in that they are influenced by the Indian Ocean [monsoons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Monsoons), usually occurring between October and March. An average of 300 mm (12 in) of rainfall occurs during this period, which is about 60% of the annual precipitation.[^cite_note-weather-152]

### Biodiversity

Main article: [Wildlife of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wildlife_of_Saudi_Arabia)



The critically endangered [Arabian leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_leopard)



The [Arabian horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_horse) is native to Arabia, and an important element of traditional Arabian folklore.

Saudi Arabia is home to five terrestrial ecoregions: [Arabian Peninsula coastal fog desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Peninsula_coastal_fog_desert), [Southwestern Arabian foothills savanna](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Arabian_foothills_savanna), [Southwestern Arabian montane woodlands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Southwestern_Arabian_montane_woodlands), [Arabian Desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_Desert), and [Red Sea Nubo-Sindian tropical desert and semi-desert](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Sea_Nubo-Sindian_tropical_desert_and_semi-desert).[^cite_note-DinersteinOlson2017-153] Wildlife includes the [Arabian leopard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_leopard),[^cite_note-Judas_et_al.,_2006-154][^cite_note-Spalton_et_al.,_2006-155] [Arabian wolf](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_wolf), [striped hyena](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Striped_hyena), [mongoose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mongoose), [baboon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Baboon), [Cape hare](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cape_hare), [sand cat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sand_cat), and [jerboa](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jerboa). Animals such as gazelles, [oryx](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oryx), leopards and [cheetahs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_cheetah)[^cite_note-Nowell1996-156] were relatively numerous until the 19th century, when extensive hunting reduced these animals almost to extinction. The culturally important [Asiatic lion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Asiatic_lion) occurred in Saudi Arabia until the late 19th century before it was hunted to extinction in the wild.[^cite_note-Nader_al1989-157] Birds include falcons (which are caught and trained for hunting), eagles, hawks, vultures, [sandgrouse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sandgrouse), and [bulbuls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bulbul). There are several species of snakes, many of which are venomous. Domesticated animals include the legendary [Arabian horse](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_horse), [Arabian camel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabian_camel), sheep, goats, cattle, donkeys, chickens, etc.

The Red Sea is a rich and diverse [ecosystem](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ecosystem) with more than [1,200 species of fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_fishes_in_the_Red_Sea)[^cite_note-fishbase-158] around 10% of which are [endemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Endemic).[^cite_note-159] This also includes 42 species of [deep water fish](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_deep_water_fish_of_the_Red_Sea).[^cite_note-fishbase-158] The rich diversity is partly owed to the 2000 km (1240 mi) of coral reef extending along the coastline; these [fringing reefs](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fringing_reef) are largely formed of stony [acropora](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Acropora) and [porites](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Porites) corals. The reefs form platforms and sometimes lagoons along the coast and occasional other features such as cylinders (such as the [Blue Hole](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Blue_Hole_\(Red_Sea\)) at [Dahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dahab)). These coastal reefs are also visited by [pelagic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pelagic_zone) species, including some of the [44 species of shark](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_sharks_in_the_Red_Sea). There are many offshore reefs including several [atolls](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Atoll). Many of the unusual offshore reef formations defy classic (i.e., Darwinian) coral reef classification schemes and are generally attributed to the high levels of tectonic activity that characterize the area.

Reflecting the country's dominant desert conditions, plant life mostly consists of herbs, plants, and shrubs that require little water. The date palm (*[Phoenix dactylifera](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Phoenix_dactylifera)*) is widespread, and is a national symbol.[^cite_note-Britannica-146]

## Government and politics

Main article: [Politics of Saudi Arabia](27272.md)



[Salman bin Abdulaziz Al Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salman_of_Saudi_Arabia)\
[King](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_of_Saudi_Arabia)



[Mohammed bin Salman](38689358.md)\
[Crown Prince](40373823.md) and [Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Prime_Minister_of_Saudi_Arabia)

Saudi Arabia is an [absolute monarchy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Absolute_monarchy);[^cite_note-Cavendish78-160] however, according to the [Basic Law of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Basic_Law_of_Saudi_Arabia) adopted by royal decree in 1992, the king must comply with [Sharia](28840.md) (Islamic law) and the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran), while the Quran and the [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) (the traditions of Muhammad) are declared to be the country's constitution.[^cite_note-Gerhard-161] No political parties or national elections are permitted.[^cite_note-Cavendish78-160] While some critics consider it to be a [totalitarian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Totalitarianism) state,[^cite_note-162][^cite_note-163] others regard it as lacking aspects of totalitarianism but nevertheless classify it as an authoritarian regime.[^cite_note-164][^cite_note-165][^cite_note-166] *[The Economist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Economist)* ranked the Saudi government 150th out of 167 in its 2022 [Democracy Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Democracy_Index),[^cite_note-167] and [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House) gave it its lowest "Not Free" rating, giving it a score of 8 out of 100 for 2023.[^cite_note-FH2019-168] In the [V-Dem Democracy Indices](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/V-Dem_Democracy_Indices), Saudi Arabia ranks amongst the lowest as a "closed autocracy," ranking at 179th and 167th out of 179 countries in electoral democracy and liberal democracy indices respectively.[^cite_note-j496-169] Additionally, according to [International IDEA](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/International_Institute_for_Democracy_and_Electoral_Assistance)'s Global State of Democracy (GSoD) Indices and Democracy Tracker, Saudi Arabia performs in the low range on overall democratic measures, with particular weaknesses in political representation, including credible elections, inclusive suffrage and effective parliament.[^cite_note-170][^cite_note-171][^cite_note-172]

In the absence of national elections and political parties,[^cite_note-Cavendish78-160] politics in Saudi Arabia takes place in two distinct arenas: within the royal family, the Al Saud, and between the royal family and the rest of Saudi society.[^cite_note-Noreng97-173] Participation in the political process is limited to a relatively small segment of the population and takes the form of the royal family consulting with the ulema, tribal sheikhs, and business elite on major decisions.[^cite_note-Britannica-146] This process is not reported by the Saudi media.[^cite_note-174]

Sadat and Khalid

[King Khalid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Khalid_of_Saudi_Arabia) (right) meeting Egypt's president [Anwar Sadat](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anwar_Sadat) with crown prince [Fahd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahd_of_Saudi_Arabia) in the background at [Cairo Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cairo_International_Airport) in 1975

By custom, all males of full age have a right to petition the king directly through the traditional tribal meeting known as the *[majlis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Majlis)*.[^cite_note-Cavendish92-175] In many ways the approach to government differs little from the traditional system of tribal rule. Tribal identity remains strong, and outside of the royal family, political influence is frequently determined by tribal affiliation. Tribal sheikhs continue to exert a considerable degree of influence over local and national events.[^cite_note-Britannica-146] In recent years there have been limited steps to widen political participation such as the establishment of the Consultative Council in the early 1990s and the National Dialogue Forum in 2003.[^cite_note-Al-Rasheed242-176] In 2005, the first municipal elections were held. In 2007, the [Allegiance Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Allegiance_Council) was created to regulate the succession.[^cite_note-Al-Rasheed242-176] In 2009, the king made significant personnel changes to the government by appointing reformers to key positions and the first woman to a ministerial post;[^cite_note-177][^cite_note-178] however, these changes have been criticized as being too slow or merely cosmetic.[^cite_note-179]

The rule of the Al Saud faces political opposition from four sources: Sunni Islamist activism; liberal critics; the [Shi'ite minority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shia_Islam_in_Saudi_Arabia)—particularly in the Eastern Province; and long-standing tribal/[regionalist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regionalism_\(politics\)) opponents (for example in the Hejaz).[^cite_note-180] Of these, the minority activists have been the most prominent threat to the government and have in recent years been involved in [violent incidents in the country](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_terrorist_incidents_in_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-Cordesman_2009-130] However, open protest against the government, even if peaceful, is not tolerated.[^cite_note-181]

### Monarchy and royal family

Main article: [House of Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/House_of_Saud)



King [Fahd](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahd_of_Saudi_Arabia) with US President [Ronald Reagan](25433.md) and future US President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) in 1985. The US and Saudi Arabia [supplied money and arms](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Operation_Cyclone) to the anti-Soviet *[mujahideen](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mujahideen)* fighters in Afghanistan.

The king combines legislative, executive, and judicial functions,[^cite_note-Britannica-146] with royal decrees forming the basis of the country's legislation.[^cite_note-Campbell-182] The prime minister presides over the [Council of Ministers of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Ministers_of_Saudi_Arabia) and [Consultative Assembly of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Consultative_Assembly_of_Saudi_Arabia). The king has usually been also the prime minister, with two exceptions: Crown Prince [Faisal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Faisal_of_Saudi_Arabia), who was prime minister during the reign of [King Saud](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saud_of_Saudi_Arabia),[^cite_note-183] and Crown Prince [Mohammed Bin Salman](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mohammed_Bin_Salman), the current prime minister since 2022.[^cite_note-184] The royal family dominates the political system. The family's vast numbers allows it to control most of the kingdom's important posts and to have an involvement at all levels of government.[^cite_note-185] The number of princes is estimated to be at least 7000, with most power and influence being wielded by the 200 or so male descendants of Ibn Saud.[^cite_note-FT_Saud-186] The key ministries are generally reserved for the royal family,[^cite_note-Cavendish78-160] as are the 13 regional governorships.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowen200715-187]



As many as 500 princes, government ministers, and business people, including Prince [Fahd bin Abdullah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fahd_bin_Abdullah_bin_Mohammed_Al_Saud), were arrested by Saudi Arabian authorities as part of the [2017 Saudi Arabian purge](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_Saudi_Arabian_purge).

The Saudi government[^cite_note-188][^cite_note-189][^cite_note-190] and the royal family[^cite_note-191][^cite_note-192][^cite_note-193] have often been accused of corruption over many years,[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbir199373Bowen2007108-194] and this continues into the 21st century.[^cite_note-195] In a country that is said to "belong" to the royal family and is named for them,[^cite_note-Kamrava-51] the lines between state assets and the personal wealth of senior princes are blurred.[^cite_note-FT_Saud-186] The extent of corruption has been described as systemic[^cite_note-196] and endemic,[^cite_note-197] and its existence was acknowledged[^cite_note-198] and defended[^cite_note-199] by [Prince Bandar bin Sultan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bandar_bin_Sultan) (a senior member of the royal family)[^cite_note-200] in an interview in 2001.[^cite_note-PBS-201]

In its [Corruption Perceptions Index](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Corruption_Perceptions_Index) for 2010, [Transparency International](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Transparency_International) gave Saudi Arabia a score of 4.7 (on a scale from 0 to 10 where 0 is "highly corrupt" and 10 is "highly clean").[^cite_note-202] Saudi Arabia has undergone a process of political and social reform, such as to increase public transparency and good governance, but nepotism and patronage are widespread when doing business in the country; the enforcement of the anti-corruption laws is selective and public officials engage in corruption with impunity. As many as 500 people, including prominent Saudi Arabian princes, government ministers, and businesspeople, were [arrested in an anti-corruption campaign](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017%E2%80%932019_Saudi_Arabian_purge) in November 2017.[^cite_note-203]

### Al ash-Sheikh and role of the ulema



[Abdullah ibn Muhammad Al ash-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdullah_ibn_Muhammad_Al_ash-Sheikh) with [Bogdan Borusewicz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bogdan_Borusewicz) in the [Polish Senate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Polish_Senate), 26 May 2014

Saudi Arabia is unique in giving the [ulema](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ulama) (the body of Islamic religious leaders and jurists) a direct role in government.[^cite_note-Goldstein118-204] The preferred ulema are of the [Salafi movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_movement). The ulema have been a key influence in major government decisions, for example the imposition of the [oil embargo in 1973](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) and the [invitation to foreign troops to Saudi Arabia in 1990](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War).[^cite_note-meforum.org-205] In addition, they have had a major role in the judicial and education systems[^cite_note-206] as well as a monopoly of authority over socio-religious affairs.[^cite_note-Hassner-207]

By the 1970s, as a result of oil wealth and the modernization initiated by King Faisal, important changes to Saudi society were underway, and the power of the ulema was in decline.[^cite_note-208] However, this changed following the [seizure of the Grand Mosque](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mosque_seizure) in Mecca in 1979 by Islamist radicals.[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbir199321-209] The government's response to the crisis included strengthening the ulema's powers and increasing their financial support:[^cite_note-Hegghammer24-122] in particular, they were given greater control over the education system[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEAbir199321-209] and were allowed to enforce the stricter observance of Wahhabi rules of moral and social behaviour.[^cite_note-Hegghammer24-122] After his accession to the throne in 2005, King Abdullah took steps to reduce the powers of the ulema, for instance transferring control over girls' education to the Ministry of Education.[^cite_note-NYT_Abdullah-210]

The ulema have historically been led by the [Al ash-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_ash-Sheikh),[^cite_note-211] the country's leading religious family.[^cite_note-Hassner-207] The Al ash-Sheikh are the descendants of [Muhammad ibn Abd al-Wahhab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhammad_ibn_Abd_al-Wahhab), the 18th-century founder of [Wahhabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism) which dominates Saudi society.[^cite_note-212] The family is second in prestige only to the Al Saud (the royal family)[^cite_note-Long11-213] with whom they formed a "mutual support pact"[^cite_note-IBP-214] and power-sharing arrangement nearly 300 years ago.[^cite_note-meforum.org-205] The pact, which persists to this day,[^cite_note-IBP-214] is based on the Al Saud maintaining the Al ash-Sheikh's authority in religious matters while upholding and propagating Wahhabi doctrine. In return, the Al ash-Sheikh support the Al Saud's political authority[^cite_note-215] thereby using its religious-[moral authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Moral_authority) to legitimize the royal family's rule.[^cite_note-216] Although the Al ash-Sheikh's domination of the ulema has diminished in recent decades,[^cite_note-Mattar-217] they still hold the most important religious posts and are closely linked to the Al Saud by a high degree of intermarriage.[^cite_note-Hassner-207]

### Legal system

Main article: [Legal system of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_system_of_Saudi_Arabia)

See also: [Crime in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crime_in_Saudi_Arabia)



Verses from the Quran. The Quran is the official constitution of the country and a primary source of law. Saudi Arabia is unique in enshrining a religious text as a political document.

[^cite_note-FOOTNOTEBowen200713-218]

The primary source of law is the Islamic [Sharia](28840.md) derived from the teachings of the [Quran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quran) and the [Sunnah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sunnah) (the traditions of Muhammad).[^cite_note-Campbell-182] Saudi Arabia is unique among modern Muslim states in that Sharia is not codified and there is no system of judicial precedent, allowing judges to use independent legal reasoning to make a decision. Thus, divergent judgments arise even in apparently identical cases,[^cite_note-Sharia_Inc_Courts-219] making predictability of legal interpretation difficult.[^cite_note-220] Saudi judges tend to follow the principles of the [Hanbali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hanbali) [school of jurisprudence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Madhhab) (*[fiqh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fiqh)*) found in pre-modern texts and noted for its literalist interpretation of the Qur'an and [hadith](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hadith).[^cite_note-Haffner58-221][^cite_note-222] In 2021, Saudi Arabia announced judicial reforms to entirely [codify its laws](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Codification_\(law\)) and eliminate discrepancies.[^cite_note-223]

Royal decrees are the other main source of law but are referred to as *regulations* rather than *laws* because they are subordinate to Sharia.[^cite_note-Campbell-182] Royal decrees supplement Sharia in areas such as labour, commercial and corporate law. Additionally, traditional tribal law and custom remain significant.[^cite_note-224] Extra-Sharia government tribunals usually handle disputes relating to specific royal decrees.[^cite_note-225] Final appeal from both Sharia courts and government tribunals is to the king, and all courts and tribunals follow Sharia rules of evidence and procedure.[^cite_note-Campbell268-226]

Retaliatory punishments, or [Qisas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas), are practised: for instance, an eye can be surgically removed at the insistence of a victim who lost his own eye.[^cite_note-Economist_Cruel-227] Families of someone unlawfully killed can choose between demanding the death penalty or granting clemency in return for a payment of [diyya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Diyya) (blood money) by the perpetrator.[^cite_note-228]

### Administrative divisions

Main articles: [Regions of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions_of_Saudi_Arabia) and [Governorates of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorates_of_Saudi_Arabia)

Saudi Arabia is divided into 13 [regions](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Regions)[^cite_note-229] ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مناطق إدارية; *manatiq idāriyya*, sing. منطقة إدارية; *[mintaqah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mintaqah) idariyya*). The regions are further divided into 118 [governorates](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Governorates_of_Saudi_Arabia) ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): محافظات; *muhafazat*, sing. محافظة; *[muhafazah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muhafazah)*). This number includes the 13 regional capitals, which have a different status as municipalities ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): أمانة; *amanah*) headed by mayors ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): أمين; *amin*). The governorates are further subdivided into sub-governorates ([Arabic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arabic_language): مراكز; *[marakiz](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Markaz_\(country_subdivision\))*, sing. مركز; *markaz*).
