### Foreign relations

Main article: [Foreign relations of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Foreign_relations_of_Saudi_Arabia)

The foreign policy of Saudi Arabia is officially focused on cooperation with the oil-exporting [Gulf States](3088555.md), the unity of the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_World), [Islamic solidarity](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ummah), and support for the [United Nations](31769.md) and multilateralism. It joined the UN in 1945[^cite_note-US_State_Dept_Saudi_Arabia-48][^cite_note-230] and is a founding member of the [Arab League](52625.md), [Gulf Cooperation Council](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cooperation_Council_for_the_Arab_States_of_the_Gulf), [Muslim World League](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Muslim_World_League), and the [Organisation of Islamic Cooperation](47725.md).[^cite_note-231][^cite_note-Saudi_Ministry_of_Foreign_Affairs-232] Saudi Arabia joined the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization) in 2005 and plays a prominent role in the [International Monetary Fund](15251.md), the [World Bank](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_World_Bank), and the [G20](2206608.md).[^cite_note-US_State_Dept_Saudi_Arabia-48] Although part of the [Non-Aligned Movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Non-Aligned_Movement), Saudi Arabia is generally considered pro-Western.[^cite_note-233]

Since co-founding [OPEC](166346.md) in 1960, Saudi Arabia's oil pricing policy has officially aimed to stabilize the world oil market and moderate sharp price movements so as not to jeopardize Western economies.[^cite_note-US_State_Dept_Saudi_Arabia-48][^cite_note-234] In 1973, Saudi Arabia and other Arab nations imposed an [oil embargo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Oil_embargo) against the United States, United Kingdom, Japan, and other Western nations that supported Israel in the [Yom Kippur War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Yom_Kippur_War).[^cite_note-235] The embargo caused an [oil crisis](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/1973_oil_crisis) with many short- and long-term effects on global politics and the global economy.[^cite_note-236]

[Saudi Arabia and the United States](9753135.md) are strategic allies;[^cite_note-BBC_News-237][^cite_note-238][^cite_note-239] the U.S. has been the most influential foreign power in the kingdom since the end of World War II.[^cite_note-240] Saudi Arabia's role in the 1991 [Gulf War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gulf_War), particularly the stationing of American troops on Saudi soil from 1991, prompted the development of a hostile Islamist response internally.[^cite_note-241] As a result, Saudi Arabia has, to some extent, distanced itself from the U.S.; for example, it refused to support or participate in the U.S.-led [invasion of Iraq](5043324.md) in 2003.[^cite_note-Britannica-146] Nevertheless, Saudi Arabia remains a key strategic ally of the U.S. and a leading importer of American arms.[^cite_note-242] In 2017, American President [Donald Trump](4848272.md) and King Salman signed a series of letters of intent for Saudi Arabia to [purchase arms from the United States](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2017_United_States%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_arms_deal) totalling \$350 billion over 10 years.[^cite_note-cnbc-20170520-243][^cite_note-244]

Since Crown Prince Mohammed bin Salman took power in 2017, Saudi Arabia has pursued closer ties with both [China](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/China%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_relations) and [Russia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Russia%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_relations) as a [balancing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Balancing_\(international_relations\)) move away from its traditional reliance on the United States. This has involved an increase in both economic and political cooperation, such as elevating Sino-Saudi relations to that of a [comprehensive strategic partnership](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_diplomatic_partnerships_of_China) as well as forming an agreement with Russia to reduce oil production among [OPEC](166346.md) nations during the [Ukraine War](70149799.md).[^cite_note-245]

Saudi Arabia has traditionally sought to preserve the regional status quo by using its financial, diplomatic, and military resources to limit the effects of revolutionary uprisings in neighbouring countries.[^cite_note-246] It has engaged in a [proxy conflict with Iran](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict) since the 1979 [Islamic Revolution](347268.md), which had called for the overthrow of monarchies and Sunni dominance. The rivalry between Iran and Saudi Arabia can be seen as the geopolitical extension of the wider Sunni-Shia conflict, with both sides being the flagship state for their respective sides. The consequences of the 2003 invasion of Iraq and the [Arab Spring](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_Spring) (2010–2012) led to increasing alarm within the Saudi monarchy over the rise of [Iran](14653.md)'s influence in the region.[^cite_note-247] These fears were reflected in comments of King Abdullah,[^cite_note-NYT_Abdullah-210] who privately urged the United States to attack Iran and "cut off the head of the snake".[^cite_note-248] However, tensions between the rivals have cooled in recent years due to diplomatic dialogue. Despite being allies with The [UAE](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/UAE) on several organizations, they have been locked in a proxy war over the [Yemeni civil war (2014–present)](46215853.md) in which the Emiratis have supported secessionists against the pro-Saudi central government.[^cite_note-249]



Major [Iran–Saudi Arabia proxy conflict](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Iran%E2%80%93Saudi_Arabia_proxy_conflict) locations Iran shown in green, KSA shown in orange

Saudi Arabia has been seen as a moderating influence in the [Arab–Israeli conflict](7960202.md), periodically putting forward a peace plan between Israel and the [Palestinians](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Palestinian_people) and condemning [Hezbollah](13919.md).[^cite_note-250]

In 2017, as part of [its nuclear power](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_power_in_Saudi_Arabia) [programme](11272657.md), Saudi Arabia planned to extract uranium domestically, taking a step towards self-sufficiency in producing [nuclear fuel](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nuclear_fuel).[^cite_note-251] Subsequently, the kingdom has been exploring foreign partnerships for constructing its first civil nuclear reactors, including with the United States, China, Russia, South Korea, and France.[^cite_note-252]

Saudi Arabia has sought to build greater relations with other Asian countries as well, particularly [India](14533.md), [Pakistan](23235.md), [South Korea](27019.md), [Indonesia](14579.md) and [Bangladesh](3454.md). Saudi Arabia sees South Korea as a key business partner in multiple core sectors and has signed a mutual defence agreement with Pakistan.[^cite_note-\:8-253] Despite having close relations with Pakistan for decades, India has emerged as another key economic partner with Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-\:8-253]

#### Allegations of sponsoring global terrorism

Main article: [Saudi Arabia and state-sponsored terrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabia_and_state-sponsored_terrorism)

Saudi Arabia has been accused of sponsoring Islamic terrorism.[^cite_note-254][^cite_note-255] According to former Iraqi Prime Minister [Nouri al-Maliki](4723094.md) in March 2014, Saudi Arabia along with Qatar provided political, financial, and media support to terrorists against the Iraqi government.[^cite_note-256] Similarly, former President of Syria [Bashar al-Assad](364813.md) noted in 2015 that the sources of the extreme ideology of the terrorist organization [ISIS](9087364.md) and other such [Salafist extremist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Salafi_jihadism) groups are the [Wahabism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wahhabism) that has been supported by the royal family of Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-257]

Relations with the U.S. became strained following [9/11 terror attacks](5058690.md).[^cite_note-258] American politicians and media accused the Saudi government of supporting terrorism and tolerating a *[jihadist](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Jihadist)* culture.[^cite_note-259] According to former U.S. Secretary of State [Hillary Clinton](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hillary_Clinton) in December 2010, "Saudi Arabia remains a critical financial support base for al-Qaida, the [Taliban](30635.md), [LeT](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LeT) and other terrorist groups... Donors in Saudi Arabia constitute the most significant source of funding to Sunni terrorist groups worldwide."[^cite_note-Guardian_05122010-260] The Saudi government denies these claims or that it exports religious or cultural extremism.[^cite_note-261] In September 2016, the U.S. Congress passed the [Justice Against Sponsors of Terrorism Act](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Justice_Against_Sponsors_of_Terrorism_Act) that would allow relatives of victims of the 11 September attacks to sue Saudi Arabia for [its government's alleged role in the attacks](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Alleged_Saudi_role_in_the_September_11_attacks).[^cite_note-262] In 2014, [Sheikh Abdulaziz bin Abdullah Al-Sheikh](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Abdulaziz_Al_Sheikh), the [Grand Mufti of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Grand_Mufti_of_Saudi_Arabia) and Chairman of the [Council of Senior Scholars](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Council_of_Senior_Scholars_\(Saudi_Arabia\)), issued a [fatwa](11259.md) explicitly prohibiting joining or supporting terrorist organizations such as [ISIS](9087364.md) and [al-Qaeda](1921.md).[^cite_note-263] According to the [U.S. Department of State](31975.md), "Saudi Arabia plays an important role in working toward a peaceful and prosperous future for the region and is a strong partner in security and counterterrorism efforts and in military, diplomatic, and financial cooperation."[^cite_note-264] A significant Saudi counterterrorism success was the foiling of a 2010 [cargo plane bomb plot](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2010_transatlantic_aircraft_bomb_plot).[^cite_note-265] In December 2015, Saudi Arabia announced the formation of the [Islamic Military Counter Terrorism Coalition](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_Military_Counter_Terrorism_Coalition), an alliance of Muslim-majority countries aimed at combating terrorism and extremism.[^cite_note-266]

Since 2016 the kingdom has started to back off from exporting [Islamic fundamentalism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Islamic_fundamentalism).[^cite_note-267] Several reforms took place including curbing the powers of [religious police](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Committee_for_the_Promotion_of_Virtue_and_the_Prevention_of_Vice_\(Saudi_Arabia\)),[^cite_note-\:4-268] and no longer funding the construction of mosques abroad.[^cite_note-269]

### Military

Main article: [Saudi Arabian Military Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_Military_Forces)

See also: [List of wars involving Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_wars_involving_Saudi_Arabia)



[M1 Abrams](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/M1_Abrams) of the [Royal Saudi Land Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Land_Forces)



[*Al-Riyadh*-class frigate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Riyadh-class_frigate) of the [Royal Saudi Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Navy)



[Eurofighter Typhoon](167667.md) of the [Royal Saudi Air Force](570367.md)

The [Armed Forces of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Armed_Forces_of_Saudi_Arabia) are under the [Ministry of Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Defence_\(Saudi_Arabia\)) and consist of the [Royal Saudi Land Forces](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_Army) (including the [Royal Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Royal_Guard_Regiment)), the [Air Force](570367.md), the [Navy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Navy), the [Air Defence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Air_Defense_Forces), and the [Strategic Missile Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Royal_Saudi_Strategic_Missile_Force); the [Saudi Arabian National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_National_Guard), which is under the [Ministry of National Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_National_Guard), is made up of tribal forces loyal to the Saudi royal family and plays a role in both domestic security and foreign defence.[^cite_note-iiss2023-270][^cite_note-CIAfactbook-271][^cite_note-\:5-272] Additionally, the kingdom has several paramilitary forces under the [Minister of Interior](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Interior_\(Saudi_Arabia\)), including the [Saudi Arabian Border Guard](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/General_Directorate_of_Border_Guard), Facilities Security Force, and Coast Guard.[^cite_note-\:5-272] The primary [special operations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special_operations) and [counterterrorism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Counter-terrorism) units are the Special Security Force and the [Emergency Force](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Emergency_Force) under the [Presidency of State Security](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Presidency_of_State_Security), which combines all counterterrorism and intelligence services and reports directly to the king. As of 2023, there are 127,000 active personnel in the Armed Forces, 130,000 in the National Guard, and 24,500 in the paramilitary security forces.

Saudi Arabia has one of the [world's highest military expenditures](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditures) in both proportional and absolute terms, at approximately US\$80.3 billion (roughly 7% of GDP) according to a 2024 estimate by the [Stockholm International Peace Research Institute](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stockholm_International_Peace_Research_Institute) (SIPRI); this places the country seventh in the world, behind the United States, China, Russia, Germany, India, and the United Kingdom. Spending on defence and security has increased significantly since the mid-1990s and was about US\$78.4 billion as of 2019.[^cite_note-\:23-273] A 2023 estimate by SIPRI ranked Saudi Arabia fourth in the world in [per capita military spending](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_with_highest_military_expenditure_per_capita). The kingdom was the world's second largest arms importer from 2019 to 2023.[^cite_note-274][^cite_note-275] According to the [BICC](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bonn_International_Centre_for_Conflict_Studies), Saudi Arabia is the 28th most militarized country in the world and possesses the second-best military equipment qualitatively in the region, after Israel.[^cite_note-276] Its modern high-technology arsenal makes Saudi Arabia among the world's most densely armed nations.[^cite_note-Library_of_Congress_2006-277]

Saudi Arabia has security relationships with the United States, the United Kingdom, and France, which provide training and weapons;[^cite_note-defensenews20152-278] the kingdom is the largest recipient of U.S. arms in the world, accounting for 12 percent of all U.S. arms exports in 2024.[^cite_note-279] Saudi Arabia has a [long-standing military relationship with Pakistan](17781104.md#Security_relations) and is speculated to have secretly funded the latter's [atomic bomb programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pakistan_and_weapons_of_mass_destruction) with the intention of purchasing its atomic weapons;[^cite_note-QV-280][^cite_note-SPO-281] in September 2025, both countries entered into a [mutual defence pact](81115024.md). Since 2018, Saudi Arabia has repeatedly expressed willingness to acquire or develop nuclear weapons in the event that its chief regional rival, Iran, develops one.[^cite_note-282][^cite_note-283]

In March 2015, Saudi Arabia mobilized 150,000 troops and 100 fighter jets to support its [intervention in the civil war](46230181.md) in neighbouring [Yemen](350939.md).[^cite_note-284][^cite_note-HRW_2015-11-26_WMT-285] By early 2016, Saudi ground forces and their coalition allies captured [Aden](38050.md) and parts of southwest Yemen, though the Houthis continued to control northern Yemen and the capital city [Sanaa](18691665.md). From there the Houthis launched successful attacks across the border into Saudi Arabia.[^cite_note-286] The Saudi military has also carried out an aerial bombing campaign and a naval blockade aimed at stopping weapons shipments to the Houthis.[^cite_note-287][^cite_note-288] As of 2024, Saudi Arabia had 2,500 troops actively participating in the conflict in Yemen.[^cite_note-289]

### Human rights

Main article: [Human rights in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Human_rights_in_Saudi_Arabia)

See also: [Capital punishment in Saudi Arabia](11817227.md)

The Saudi government, which mandates Muslim and non-Muslim observance of Sharia law under the absolute rule of the House of Saud, has been denounced by international organizations and governments for violating human rights.[^cite_note-290] The authoritarian regime is consistently ranked among the "worst of the worst" in [Freedom House](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_House)'s [annual survey of political and civil rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Freedom_in_the_World).[^cite_note-291] According to [Amnesty International](18947898.md), security forces torture and ill-treat detainees to extract confessions to be used as evidence against them.[^cite_note-292] Saudi Arabia abstained from the UN vote adopting the [Universal Declaration of Human Rights](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Universal_Declaration_of_Human_Rights), saying it contradicted Sharia.[^cite_note-293] Mass executions, such as those carried out [in 2016](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2016_Saudi_Arabia_mass_execution),[^cite_note-294] [2019](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2019_Saudi_Arabia_mass_execution),[^cite_note-wsj-295] [2022](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/2022_Saudi_Arabia_mass_execution),[^cite_note-apnews1-296] and 2025[^cite_note-297] have been condemned by international rights groups.

Since 2001, Saudi Arabia has engaged in [internet censorship](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Censorship_in_Saudi_Arabia#The_Internet). Most censorship falls into two categories: "immoral" content (mostly pornographic and LGBT-supportive sites along with sites promoting any religion other than Sunni Islam) and blacklisted political content critical of the regime, as dictated by [Saudi Arabia's Ministry of Media](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Media_\(Saudi_Arabia\)).[^cite_note-298][^cite_note-299][^cite_note-300]



[Deera Square](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Deera_Square), central Riyadh. It is a former site of public beheadings.

[^cite_note-301]

Saudi Arabian law does not recognize [sexual orientations](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LGBT_rights_in_Saudi_Arabia) or religious freedom, and the public practice of non-Muslim religions is actively prohibited.[^cite_note-302] The justice system regularly engages in [capital punishment](5902.md), which has included public executions by [decapitation](70802.md).[^cite_note-303][^cite_note-304] In line with Sharia in the [Saudi justice system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Legal_system_of_Saudi_Arabia), the death penalty can theoretically be imposed for a range of offences,[^cite_note-305] including murder, rape, armed robbery, repeated drug use, [apostasy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apostasy),[^cite_note-BBCexecutioner-306] adultery,[^cite_note-FRD306-307] and [witchcraft](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Witchcraft).[^cite_note-Miethe-308] It can be carried out by beheading with a sword,[^cite_note-BBCexecutioner-306] [stoning](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Stoning) or by firing squad,[^cite_note-FRD306-307] followed by [crucifixion](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Crucifixion#Legal_execution_in_Islamic_states) (exposure of the body after execution).[^cite_note-Miethe-308] In 2022, the Saudi Crown Prince stated that capital punishments will be removed "except for one category mentioned in the Quran", namely [homicide](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Qisas#Traditional_jurisprudence), under which certain conditions must be applied.[^cite_note-apnews1-296] In April 2020, Saudi Supreme Court issued a directive to eliminate the punishment of flogging from the court system, being replaced by imprisonment or fines.[^cite_note-309][^cite_note-310]

Historically, Saudi women have faced [discrimination](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Discrimination) in many aspects of their lives and were effectively treated as [legal minors](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Minor_\(law\)) under the [male guardianship system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wali_\(Islamic_legal_guardian\)).[^cite_note-HRW2016-311] The treatment of women has been referred to as "[sex segregation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sex_segregation)"[^cite_note-312][^cite_note-313] and "gender [apartheid](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Apartheid)".[^cite_note-Handrahan-314][^cite_note-315] Since the 2000s, the kingdom has made several attempts to improve the status of women in order to elevate their global standing. As of June 2023, the kingdom has reportedly reversed its ban on women "becoming lawyers, engineers, or geologists" and established "aggressive [affirmative action](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Affirmative_action) programmes", doubling the female labour force participation rate. It has added "its first female newspaper editors, diplomats, TV anchors and public prosecutors", with a female head of the Saudi stock exchange and member on the board of [Saudi Aramco](290527.md).[^cite_note-GFOELLER_5_June_2023-316] In June 2018, the Saudi government issued a law allowing women to drive.[^cite_note-317] However, an investigation by *[The Guardian](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Guardian)* newspaper in 2025 found that the *[Dar al-Reaya](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dar_al-Reaya)*, officially "care homes", continued to be effectively "jails" for women whose families wished to institutionalize them for disobedience, extramarital sexual relations or being absent from home. Some women are sent to Dar al-Reaya to protect a family's reputation after a woman is sexually abused by a brother or father. Treatment was reported to be "hellish": with solitary confinement, flogging, and no family contact.[^cite_note-levitt-318]

Saudi Arabia is a notable destination country for men and women trafficked for the purposes of [slave](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery) labour and sexual exploitation.[^cite_note-factbook-319] Migrants from Asia and Africa are employed in the country's construction, hospitality, and domestic work sectors under the [kafala system](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kafala_system), which human rights groups say is linked to [slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Slavery_in_Saudi_Arabia).[^cite_note-320][^cite_note-321]

## Economy

Main article: [Economy of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_Saudi_Arabia)

With [gross domestic product (GDP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nominal_GDP) at over \$1.1 trillion nominally, and more than \$2.3 trillion by [purchasing power parity (PPP)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Purchasing_power_parity), Saudi Arabia has the second largest [economy in the Middle East](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Economy_of_the_Middle_East) (after [Turkey](11125639.md)), the largest in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world), and the 18th largest in the world.[^cite_note-imf2-322] It has the world's [second-largest proven reserves of petroleum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_proven_oil_reserves), of which it is [third largest producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_oil_production) and the [largest exporter](17808102.md);[^cite_note-autogenerated1995-323][^cite_note-324] the country also has the [sixth-largest proven natural gas reserves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/List_of_countries_by_natural_gas_proven_reserves).[^cite_note-\:0-40] Saudi Arabia is considered an "[energy superpower](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Energy_superpower),"[^cite_note-undp-325][^cite_note-326] having the third highest total estimated value of natural resources, valued at US\$34.4 trillion in 2016.[^cite_note-327]

Saudi Arabia has a [command economy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Command_economy) based largely on petroleum; the oil industry accounts for roughly 63%[^cite_note-328] of budget revenue, 67%[^cite_note-329] of export earnings, and 45% of nominal GDP compared with 40% from the private sector. It is strongly dependent on foreign workers, with about 80% of private sector employees being non-Saudi.[^cite_note-coy-employ-330][^cite_note-McDowall-331] Challenges to the economy include halting or reversing the decline in per-capita income, improving education to prepare youth for the workforce and providing them with employment, diversifying the economy, stimulating the private sector and housing construction, and diminishing corruption and inequality.[^cite_note-HT2009:_150-332]

The [Organization of Petroleum Exporting Countries](166346.md) (OPEC) limits its members' oil production based on their "proven reserves". Saudi Arabia's published reserves have shown little change since 1980, with the main exception being an increase of about 100 billion barrels (1.6×10<sup>10</sup> m<sup>3</sup>) between 1987 and 1988.[^cite_note-333] [Matthew Simmons](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Matthew_Simmons) has suggested that Saudi Arabia is greatly exaggerating its reserves and may soon show production declines (see [peak oil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Peak_oil)).[^cite_note-334]



Map of [oil](23195.md) and [gas](22131.md) pipelines in the Middle-East

From 2003 to 2013, "several key services" were privatized—municipal water supply, electricity, telecommunications—and parts of education and health care, traffic control and car accident reporting were also privatized. According to Arab News columnist Abdel Aziz Aluwaisheg, "in almost every one of these areas, consumers have raised serious concerns about the performance of these privatized entities."[^cite_note-Aluwaisheg-335] In November 2005, Saudi Arabia was approved as a member of the [World Trade Organization](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/World_Trade_Organization). Negotiations to join had focused on the degree to which Saudi Arabia is willing to increase market access to foreign goods and in 2000, the government established the [Saudi Arabian General Investment Authority](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_General_Investment_Authority) to encourage foreign direct investment in the kingdom. Saudi Arabia maintains a list of sectors in which foreign investment is prohibited, but the government plans to open some closed sectors such as telecommunications, insurance, and power transmission/distribution over time. The government has also made an attempt at "[Saudizing](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudization)" the economy, replacing foreign workers with Saudi nationals with limited success.[^cite_note-house-161-336]



The *[hajj](23487999.md)* is an annual [Islamic](6037917.md) [pilgrimage](23763.md) to [Mecca](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mecca), the [holiest city](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Holiest_sites_in_Islam) for Muslims.

[^cite_note-Modarresi-337]

In addition to petroleum and gas, Saudi has a significant gold mining sector in the [Mahd adh Dhahab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahd_adh_Dhahab) region and significant other mineral industries, an agricultural sector (especially in the southwest) based on vegetables, fruits, dates etc. and livestock, and large number of temporary jobs created by the roughly two million annual *[hajj](23487999.md)* pilgrims.[^cite_note-HT2009:_150-332] Saudi Arabia has had five-year "Development Plans" since 1970. Among its plans were to launch "economic cities" (e.g. [King Abdullah Economic City](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/King_Abdullah_Economic_City)) to diversify the economy and provide jobs.

Saudi Arabia is increasingly activating its ports in order to participate in trade between Europe and China in addition to oil transport. To this end, ports such as Jeddah Islamic Port or King Abdullah Economic City are being rapidly expanded, and investments are being made in logistics. The country is historically and currently part of the [Maritime Silk Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Maritime_Silk_Road).[^cite_note-338][^cite_note-339][^cite_note-340][^cite_note-341]

Statistics on poverty in the kingdom are not available through the UN resources because the Saudi government does not issue any.[^cite_note-342] The Saudi state discourages calling attention to or complaining about poverty. In December 2011, the Saudi interior ministry arrested three reporters and held them for almost two weeks for questioning after they uploaded a video on the topic to YouTube.[^cite_note-343][^cite_note-344][^cite_note-345] Authors of the video claim that 22% of Saudis may be considered poor.[^cite_note-346] Observers researching the issue prefer to stay anonymous[^cite_note-347] because of the risk of being arrested.

The unexpected impact of the [COVID-19 pandemic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/COVID-19_pandemic_in_Saudi_Arabia) on the economy, along with Saudi Arabia's poor human rights records, laid unforeseen challenges before the development plans of the kingdom, where some of the programmes under '[Vision 2030](50299103.md)' were also expected to be affected.[^cite_note-348] In May 2020, the [Finance Minister of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ministry_of_Finance_\(Saudi_Arabia\)) admitted that the country's economy was facing a severe economical crisis for the first time in decades, because of the pandemic as well as declining global oil markets.

In July 2024 Saudi Arabia's Renewable Energy Localisation Company (RELC) has formed three joint ventures with Chinese companies to advance the kingdom's clean energy infrastructure. As part of Saudi Arabia's 2030 targets, the [Public Investment Fund](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Public_Investment_Fund) is actively promoting the localization of renewable energy components. RELC, a division of the sovereign fund, facilitates partnerships between global manufacturers and Saudi private sector firms to strengthen local supply chains. These initiatives aim to localize up to 75% of the components used in Saudi Arabia's renewable projects by 2030, positioning the country as a major global exporter of renewable technologies.[^cite_note-349]

### Agriculture

Main article: [Agriculture in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Agriculture_in_Saudi_Arabia)

&#x20;\
&#x20;Saudi Arabia's predominantly arid environment, poor soil, and traditional nomadic lifestyle have long favoured [pastoralism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Pastoralism), particularly of camels, sheep, and goats; agricultural production has historically been localized and oriented towards [basic subsistence](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subsistence_agriculture).[^cite_note-\:6-350] Less than two percent of the total land area is used for crops, with agriculture constituting only a fraction of GDP (2.5 percent) and employing a similar proportion of the workforce.[^cite_note-351][^cite_note-\:6-350]

Initial attempts to develop dairy farming on a commercial scale occurred in the [Al Kharj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al_Kharj) District south of Riyadh during the 1950s.[^cite_note-352] Serious large-scale agricultural development began in the 1970s,[^cite_note-353] particularly with wheat,[^cite_note-354] with the government launching an extensive programme to promote modern farming technology; establish rural roads, irrigation networks and storage and export facilities; and encourage agricultural research and training institutions. The kingdom has continued prioritizing domestic food production through subsidies and generous incentives.[^cite_note-\:6-350]

Owing to these efforts, there has been substantial growth in the production of all basic foods: Although it still imports the bulk of its food needs, Saudi Arabia is self-sufficient in several commodities, particularly wheat, meat, milk, and eggs. The country exports dates, dairy products, eggs, fish, poultry, fruits, vegetables, and flowers. Dates, which are indigenous to Saudi Arabia and a traditional staple of its diet, are now mainly grown for global humanitarian aid; the kingdom is the world's [second largest producer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Date_palm#Production) after Egypt, producing 1.6 million tonnes as of 2022. Another indigenous crop, the olive, is also heavily cultivated: The [Al Jouf region](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Jawf_Province) has millions of olive trees—including the world's largest olive farm at 7,713 hectares[^cite_note-355]—and the number is expected to increase to 20 million trees.[^cite_note-356] Saudi farmers also grow substantial amounts of other grains such as barley, sorghum, and millet.

Saudi Arabia has some of the most modern and largest dairy farms in the Middle East; milk production boasts a productive annual rate of 6,800 litres (1,800 US gallons) per cow, one of the highest in the world. Saudi dairy company [Almarai](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Almarai) is the largest vertically integrated dairy company in the Middle East,[^cite_note-357] and the largest dairy firm in the world by volume;[^cite_note-358] it operates the world's largest dairy farm at Al Safi.[^cite_note-359]

Following an end to domestic wheat production in 2016 to preserve water,[^cite_note-360] Saudi Arabia encouraged small famers to produce wheat in 2019 through a domestic production programme established under the Saudi Ministry of Environment, Water, and Agriculture (MEWA).[^cite_note-\:7-361] In 2025, wheat production is forecast to more than double the five-year average.[^cite_note-362]

Consuming nonrenewable groundwater resulted in the loss of an estimated four-fifths of the total groundwater reserves by 2012.[^cite_note-363] About half the arable land is cultivated through [rain-fed dry farming](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Dryland_farming) (mostly in [Asir](468723.md)), two-fifths through the planting of local tree crops (namely dates and olives), and the remainder by irrigation, mostly by underground water.

As part of the Saudi Green Initiative, which seeks to restore vegetation cover and ecological sustainability, the kingdom aims to plant 100 million [mangroves](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mangrove); as of 2024, the National Centre for Vegetation Cover Development and Combating Desertification announced it had planted 13 million seedlings.[^cite_note-364]

### Water supply and sanitation

Main articles: [Water supply and sanitation in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Water_supply_and_sanitation_in_Saudi_Arabia) and [Irrigation in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Irrigation_in_Saudi_Arabia)



[Al-Musk Lake](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Al-Musk_Lake) close to Jeddah

One of the main challenges for Saudi Arabia is [water scarcity](15380061.md). Substantial investments have been undertaken in seawater [desalination](156787.md), water distribution, sewerage and [wastewater treatment](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wastewater_treatment). Today about 50% of drinking water comes from desalination, 40% from the mining of non-renewable groundwater, and 10% from surface water in the mountainous southwest of the country.[^cite_note-365] Saudi Arabia is suffering from a major depletion of the water in its underground aquifers and a resultant break down and disintegration of its agriculture as a consequence.[^cite_note-366][^cite_note-367] As a result of the catastrophe, Saudi Arabia has bought agricultural land in the United States,[^cite_note-368][^cite_note-369] Argentina,[^cite_note-370] and Africa.[^cite_note-371][^cite_note-372][^cite_note-373][^cite_note-374] Saudi Arabia ranked as a major buyer of agricultural land in foreign countries.[^cite_note-375][^cite_note-376]



Saudi Arabia was the third most water stressed country in the world in 2022.

[^cite_note-377]

According to the [Joint Monitoring Programme](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Joint_Monitoring_Programme_for_Water_Supply_and_Sanitation) (JMP) for Water Supply and Sanitation of the [WHO](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/WHO) and [UNICEF](19698110.md), the latest reliable source on access to water and sanitation in Saudi Arabia is the 2004 census. It indicates that 97% of the population had access to an improved source of drinking water and 99% had access to [improved sanitation](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Improved_sanitation). For 2015, the JMP estimates that access to sanitation increased to 100%. Sanitation was primarily through on-site solutions, and about 40% of the population was connected to sewers.[^cite_note-JMP-378] In 2015, 886,000 people lacked access to "improved" water.[^cite_note-379][^cite_note-380]

### Tourism

Main article: [Tourism in Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tourism_in_Saudi_Arabia)

&#x20;\
&#x20;In 2019, Saudi Arabia adopted a general tourism [travel visa](330265.md) to allow non-Muslims to visit.[^cite_note-381] Although most tourism largely involves religious pilgrimages, there is growth in the leisure tourism sector. According to the [World Bank](36752.md), approximately 14.3 million people visited Saudi Arabia in 2012, making it the world's 19th-most-visited country.[^cite_note-382] Tourism is an important component of the [Saudi Vision 2030](50299103.md), and according to a report conducted by [BMI Research](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BMI_Research) in 2018 both religious and non-religious tourism have significant potential for expansion.[^cite_note-383]

The kingdom offers an electronic visa for foreign visitors to attend sports events and concerts.[^cite_note-384] In 2019, the kingdom announced its plans to open visa applications for visitors, where people from about 50 countries would be able to get tourist visas to Saudi.[^cite_note-385] In 2020 it was announced that holders of a US, UK or [Schengen](16565139.md) visa are eligible for a Saudi electronic visa upon arrival.[^cite_note-386]

## Demographics

Main articles: [Saudi Arabian people](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Saudi_Arabian_people) and [Demographics of Saudi Arabia](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Demographics_of_Saudi_Arabia)



Saudi Arabia population density (people per km

2

)

Saudi Arabia's reported population is 32,175,224 as of 2022,[^cite_note-387] making it the fourth most populous country in the [Arab world](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab_world).[^cite_note-388] Close to 42% of its inhabitants are immigrants,[^cite_note-CIA2-389] mostly from the Middle East, South Asia, and Africa.[^cite_note-390]

The Saudi population has grown rapidly since 1950, when it was estimated at 3 million.[^cite_note-391] For much of the 20th century, the country had one of the highest population growth rates in the world, at around 3% annually;[^cite_note-long-27-392] it continues to grow at a rate of 1.62% per year,[^cite_note-CIA2-389] slightly higher than the rest of the Middle East and North Africa. Consequently, the Saudi [people are quite young](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Youth_in_Saudi_Arabia) by global standards, with over half the population under 25 years old.[^cite_note-393]

Ethnically, 90% of citizens are [Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Arab) with 10% holding [Afro-Arab](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Afro-Arab) ancestry.[^cite_note-394] Most Saudis are concentrated in the southwest; Hejaz, which is the most populated region,[^cite_note-395] is home to one-third of the population, followed by neighbouring Najd (28%) and the Eastern Province (15%).[^cite_note-396] As late as 1970, most Saudis lived a subsistence life in the rural provinces, but in the last half of the 20th century, the kingdom has urbanized rapidly: as of 2023, about 85% of Saudis live in urban metropolitan areas—specifically Riyadh, Jeddah, and Dammam.[^cite_note-397][^cite_note-398] As recently as the early 1960s, Saudi Arabia's slave population was estimated at 300000.[^cite_note-399] [Slavery](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_of_slavery_in_the_Muslim_world) was officially abolished in 1962.[^cite_note-autogenerated1-400][^cite_note-autogenerated2-401]
